I’ll mean to be quick, but I take too long and then I’m late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or try to fix the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “something bad” exactly would happen, like… nothing huge, I guess. But it feels like people will notice it if I don’t fix it, especially at work when I’m standing there under those lights all day.\n\nSo on breaks I usually end up in the bathroom checking it in the mirror, turning my face different ways, and fixing my hair around that side. And then I’ll go back to the register and see girls come through who just look normal and pretty, and I start comparing again. Sometimes I get so stuck trying to make it look better that I’m late getting back, which is embarrassing because then I feel like everyone knows I was in there too long.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking or trying to fix the uneven spot on your face, are you trying to make it perfect or are you trying to prevent something bad from happening because of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I’m trying to make it look… okay enough, I guess. Not perfect-perfect, but if I can see it in the bathroom mirror and move my hair around it, then I feel like maybe it’s not as obvious.\n\nLike on my breaks I’ll go check my face from different angles, and then I start messing with my hair around that side. And sometimes I’ll come back to the register and see girls in line who look really put together, and then I start comparing again. I don’t mean to take that long, but sometimes I do and then I’m late getting back, which makes me feel even worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing the uneven spot on your face helps reduce your anxiety about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… for a little bit it does. Like when I go on my break and check it in the bathroom mirror, if I can fix my hair around that side, I feel a little less panicky or like maybe it’s not so obvious.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really last. I’ll go back to the register and start noticing girls coming through my line and comparing my face to theirs, and then I’m kind of right back in it. Sometimes I spend so long in the bathroom messing with it that I’m late getting back, which then makes me stressed for a whole different reason.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about the uneven spot on your face, are you more concerned about the way it actually looks or about what other people might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… both, kind of? Like I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially in photos or under the store lights. And I know that sounds small, but when I see it, it feels really obvious.\n\nOther people say they don’t see it, or they’re like, “What are you talking about?” but I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or even my managers notice when they look at me. So I guess it’s about how it looks, but mostly because I’m worried everyone else is seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about the uneven spot on your face, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you're afraid of what others might think of you as a person?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more that it makes me feel unattractive, but then my brain goes right to, like, customers and managers noticing it too. Like that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek — especially in photos or under the store lights — I get stuck on it.\n\nAnd people say they don’t see it, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. It still feels like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me. I know that sounds kind of dramatic for something small, but… yeah.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about what others might think of the uneven spot on your face, is it because you're afraid they'll judge you as a person or just that they'll notice the spot?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that they’ll notice it first, like… that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I keep getting stuck on it, especially in photos or under the store lights at work.\n\nI don’t know if I think they’re judging me as a whole person exactly. It’s more like, if customers or managers look at me, I feel like that’s what their eyes go to, even when people tell me they don’t see anything. Like my friend said once that the lighting in those stores makes everyone look weird, and I know that’s probably true, but then I’m standing there at the register and I just keep thinking, “They can see it.”\n\nDoctor: When you worry about what others might think of the uneven spot on your face, is it because you're afraid they'll think less of you as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t really think, like, “they’ll think I’m a bad person” or something. It’s more… I get stuck on them seeing it, and then I start feeling embarrassed.\n\nLike at work, when someone is standing close at the register, I’ll suddenly think about the lighting and my face instead of what they’re buying. Which is annoying because then I’m trying to be normal and scan stuff, and my manager’s nearby, and I’m thinking, “Do they notice?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking less of me as a person. Maybe not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would like you less if they noticed the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not exactly like they’d like me less, but it feels like once they notice it, that’s all they’re seeing. Like the tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek — in photos it jumps out to me, and under the store lights at work it feels even worse.\n\nI’ll be helping a customer and suddenly I’m thinking about whether they can see it, or if my manager notices when they walk by. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it. It just feels like it’s the first thing people notice about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would view you differently as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “as a person,” exactly. I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially in photos or under the store lights at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and someone’s buying gum or whatever, and I’m supposed to be paying attention, but I’m thinking, “That’s what they’re noticing first.”\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like customers and managers do. So maybe I worry they view me differently, but mostly it’s that it feels like the spot becomes the main thing about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would dislike you or think you're flawed as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d dislike me, exactly. It’s more like… I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, like in photos or when I’m under those really bright store lights. I’ll be at the register and then suddenly I’m not even thinking about the customer, I’m thinking, “They’re looking at it.”\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. Like it kind of becomes the main thing about my face, even if that sounds dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less attractive to others if the uneven spot on your face was more noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if other people would actually think that, but if it was more noticeable, I feel like I’d look worse to them. Like that uneven spot around my nose and cheek would be the first thing they saw.\n\nIt’s weird because people tell me they don’t really see it, but then I’m at work under those bright store lights, and I start thinking customers or my manager are noticing it. And then I get stuck on it, like especially if I’ve seen a photo of myself that day and it looked bad. So… yeah, I guess I worry it would make me less attractive, even if maybe other people wouldn’t say that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So… yeah, I guess I worry it would make me less attractive, even if maybe other people wouldn’t say that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your worth as a person would be affected if people noticed the uneven spot on your face?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek in photos and under the store lights, and even when other people say they don't see it, it feels like it's the first thing customers and managers notice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think my worth, like… as a whole person, should depend on it. I know that sounds like the “right” answer. But when I get stuck on it, it kind of feels bigger than that.

Like I came in because I keep fixating on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially if I see it in a photo or I’m under those bright store lights at work. And then I’m supposed to be scanning groceries or making change, and I’m thinking about whether the customer is noticing it. Or if my manager walks by, I’m wondering if that’s what they see first.

So I don’t know if it affects my worth exactly, but it makes me feel… less okay, I guess. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing people notice.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this little uneven spot, like around my nose and cheek. It’s especially bad in photos or under the lights at work—the store lights are kind of harsh, and I’m a cashier so I’m just standing there with people looking at me all day. \n\nOther people say they don’t really see anything, but it feels like it’s the first thing customers notice, or my managers notice when they come over. And then I start thinking about it a lot. Sorry, I know that sounds small… I just can’t seem to stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you get stuck on, or are there other things that you also find yourself obsessing over?\n\nPatient: Mostly my appearance, yeah. I mean, I guess I can worry about normal stuff too, like if I’m late for work or if my drawer is off at the register—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. The thing I get stuck on is that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. Like in photos, or under the store lights, it just jumps out at me. Even when people say they don’t see it, I feel like customers and managers notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, at least at work. Like, my manager has noticed I’m coming back late from breaks, because I’ll get stuck checking it in the bathroom mirror or trying to fix how I look before going back out.\n\nAnd then at the register I feel like I’m moving slower, which is bad because I’m trying to get promoted, and now I’m worried I just look unreliable. I mean, they already have favorites there, you know, like people who can chat with customers and still scan everything fast… but yeah, it’s mostly that I’m scared this is messing that up for me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about the uneven spot on your face ever pop into your head when you're not actively looking at yourself or thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… like even if I’m not actually looking in a mirror, I’ll still suddenly think about it. Like if I’m at the register and the store lights hit weird, I’ll just remember that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek and then I’m sure it’s what people are seeing.\n\nIt’s worse in photos too, which is probably why I hate when coworkers want to take pictures after a shift—one of them is always doing that for socials, and I try to stand at a certain angle. But, um… yeah, the thought does pop in, even when I’m not trying to think about it. People say they don’t notice it, but it feels like customers and managers notice it first.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to fix or check the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. On my breaks I’ll go to the bathroom and check it in the mirror, like from different angles, and then I’ll try to move my hair around so it kind of covers that side better.\n\nAnd then I get kind of stuck there longer than I mean to. Sometimes I’m like, “Okay, just one more second,” but then I’m checking again. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line, which feels embarrassing to say, but I do it. And sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the uneven spot on your face, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I tell myself I can stop, like, “just don’t look this time,” but then on my break I end up in the bathroom mirror anyway, checking my face from different angles and fixing my hair around it.\n\nIt’s kind of like when customers come through my line and some girls just look really put together without trying, and then I start comparing myself, which is stupid because I’m supposed to be scanning stuff and keeping the line moving. But yeah… sometimes I take so long trying to check it or cover it that I’m late getting back to the register. So I guess it doesn’t feel totally optional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable if you can't check the uneven spot on your face when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I get pretty uncomfortable. Like if I’m stuck at the register and there’s a line, I’ll start thinking, “I just need to check it real quick,” and then it’s hard to focus.\n\nUsually on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror and looking at my face from different angles, fixing my hair around that side. And then sometimes I compare myself to girls who come through my line, which is—ugh, I know it’s not helpful. I’ll mean to be quick, but I take too long and then I’m late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or try to fix the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “something bad” exactly would happen, like… nothing huge, I guess. But it feels like people will notice it if I don’t fix it, especially at work when I’m standing there under those lights all day.\n\nSo on breaks I usually end up in the bathroom checking it in the mirror, turning my face different ways, and fixing my hair around that side. And then I’ll go back to the register and see girls come through who just look normal and pretty, and I start comparing again. Sometimes I get so stuck trying to make it look better that I’m late getting back, which is embarrassing because then I feel like everyone knows I was in there too long.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking or trying to fix the uneven spot on your face, are you trying to make it perfect or are you trying to prevent something bad from happening because of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I’m trying to make it look… okay enough, I guess. Not perfect-perfect, but if I can see it in the bathroom mirror and move my hair around it, then I feel like maybe it’s not as obvious.\n\nLike on my breaks I’ll go check my face from different angles, and then I start messing with my hair around that side. And sometimes I’ll come back to the register and see girls in line who look really put together, and then I start comparing again. I don’t mean to take that long, but sometimes I do and then I’m late getting back, which makes me feel even worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing the uneven spot on your face helps reduce your anxiety about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… for a little bit it does. Like when I go on my break and check it in the bathroom mirror, if I can fix my hair around that side, I feel a little less panicky or like maybe it’s not so obvious.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really last. I’ll go back to the register and start noticing girls coming through my line and comparing my face to theirs, and then I’m kind of right back in it. Sometimes I spend so long in the bathroom messing with it that I’m late getting back, which then makes me stressed for a whole different reason.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about the uneven spot on your face, are you more concerned about the way it actually looks or about what other people might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… both, kind of? Like I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially in photos or under the store lights. And I know that sounds small, but when I see it, it feels really obvious.\n\nOther people say they don’t see it, or they’re like, “What are you talking about?” but I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or even my managers notice when they look at me. So I guess it’s about how it looks, but mostly because I’m worried everyone else is seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about the uneven spot on your face, is it more because you think it makes you look unattractive or because you're afraid of what others might think of you as a person?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more that it makes me feel unattractive, but then my brain goes right to, like, customers and managers noticing it too. Like that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek — especially in photos or under the store lights — I get stuck on it.\n\nAnd people say they don’t see it, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. It still feels like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me. I know that sounds kind of dramatic for something small, but… yeah.\n\nDoctor: When you worry about what others might think of the uneven spot on your face, is it because you're afraid they'll judge you as a person or just that they'll notice the spot?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that they’ll notice it first, like… that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I keep getting stuck on it, especially in photos or under the store lights at work.\n\nI don’t know if I think they’re judging me as a whole person exactly. It’s more like, if customers or managers look at me, I feel like that’s what their eyes go to, even when people tell me they don’t see anything. Like my friend said once that the lighting in those stores makes everyone look weird, and I know that’s probably true, but then I’m standing there at the register and I just keep thinking, “They can see it.”\n\nDoctor: When you worry about what others might think of the uneven spot on your face, is it because you're afraid they'll think less of you as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t really think, like, “they’ll think I’m a bad person” or something. It’s more… I get stuck on them seeing it, and then I start feeling embarrassed.\n\nLike at work, when someone is standing close at the register, I’ll suddenly think about the lighting and my face instead of what they’re buying. Which is annoying because then I’m trying to be normal and scan stuff, and my manager’s nearby, and I’m thinking, “Do they notice?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as thinking less of me as a person. Maybe not exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would like you less if they noticed the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Maybe… not exactly like they’d like me less, but it feels like once they notice it, that’s all they’re seeing. Like the tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek — in photos it jumps out to me, and under the store lights at work it feels even worse.\n\nI’ll be helping a customer and suddenly I’m thinking about whether they can see it, or if my manager notices when they walk by. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I don’t really believe it. It just feels like it’s the first thing people notice about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would view you differently as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “as a person,” exactly. I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially in photos or under the store lights at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and someone’s buying gum or whatever, and I’m supposed to be paying attention, but I’m thinking, “That’s what they’re noticing first.”\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like customers and managers do. So maybe I worry they view me differently, but mostly it’s that it feels like the spot becomes the main thing about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would dislike you or think you're flawed as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d dislike me, exactly. It’s more like… I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, like in photos or when I’m under those really bright store lights. I’ll be at the register and then suddenly I’m not even thinking about the customer, I’m thinking, “They’re looking at it.”\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. Like it kind of becomes the main thing about my face, even if that sounds dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less attractive to others if the uneven spot on your face was more noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if other people would actually think that, but if it was more noticeable, I feel like I’d look worse to them. Like that uneven spot around my nose and cheek would be the first thing they saw.\n\nIt’s weird because people tell me they don’t really see it, but then I’m at work under those bright store lights, and I start thinking customers or my manager are noticing it. And then I get stuck on it, like especially if I’ve seen a photo of myself that day and it looked bad. So… yeah, I guess I worry it would make me less attractive, even if maybe other people wouldn’t say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person would be affected if people noticed the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my worth, like… as a whole person, should depend on it. I know that sounds like the “right” answer. But when I get stuck on it, it kind of feels bigger than that.\n\nLike I came in because I keep fixating on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially if I see it in a photo or I’m under those bright store lights at work. And then I’m supposed to be scanning groceries or making change, and I’m thinking about whether the customer is noticing it. Or if my manager walks by, I’m wondering if that’s what they see first.\n\nSo I don’t know if it affects my worth exactly, but it makes me feel… less okay, I guess. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing people notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It just feels like it’s the first thing people notice about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would view you differently as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “as a person,” exactly. I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially in photos or under the store lights at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and someone’s buying gum or whatever, and I’m supposed to be paying attention, but I’m thinking, “That’s what they’re noticing first.”\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like customers and managers do. So maybe I worry they view me differently, but mostly it’s that it feels like the spot becomes the main thing about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would dislike you or think you're flawed as a person if they saw the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’d dislike me, exactly. It’s more like… I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, like in photos or when I’m under those really bright store lights. I’ll be at the register and then suddenly I’m not even thinking about the customer, I’m thinking, “They’re looking at it.”\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. Like it kind of becomes the main thing about my face, even if that sounds dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less attractive to others if the uneven spot on your face was more noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if other people would actually think that, but if it was more noticeable, I feel like I’d look worse to them. Like that uneven spot around my nose and cheek would be the first thing they saw.\n\nIt’s weird because people tell me they don’t really see it, but then I’m at work under those bright store lights, and I start thinking customers or my manager are noticing it. And then I get stuck on it, like especially if I’ve seen a photo of myself that day and it looked bad. So… yeah, I guess I worry it would make me less attractive, even if maybe other people wouldn’t say that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worth as a person would be affected if people noticed the uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my worth, like… as a whole person, should depend on it. I know that sounds like the “right” answer. But when I get stuck on it, it kind of feels bigger than that.\n\nLike I came in because I keep fixating on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, especially if I see it in a photo or I’m under those bright store lights at work. And then I’m supposed to be scanning groceries or making change, and I’m thinking about whether the customer is noticing it. Or if my manager walks by, I’m wondering if that’s what they see first.\n\nSo I don’t know if it affects my worth exactly, but it makes me feel… less okay, I guess. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing people notice."
